# EXPANSION CW125-03 — Names in the Dark

## A prose-first memorial expansion about the remembered claim, the people who inherit it, and the blanks that remain human.

### Prose Wave 125: What a Warning Cannot Finish

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with full scene, attributable writing, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 beat pairings; four alternative authored forms for each pairing; 192 candidates, of which a verified consumer should select only what it can support.
**Current local anchor:** memorial_raider_attack/names_in_the_dark — Raider Attack Victims.
**Tone:** A memorial whose brave public account is read beside the harder question of what tribute, fear, and collective survival leave unspoken.
**Scope:** narrative prose only; no production code, authoritative JSON edits, mechanics, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save change.

## 1. Expansion thesis

This plan explores A memorial whose brave public account is read beside the harder question of what tribute, fear, and collective survival leave unspoken. It uses the source text “They fought to the last. Raiders came in the night, and these brave souls stood between us and annihilation. We remember their names in the dark.” as a narrow canon anchor, not as an excuse to manufacture witnesses or explain every silence. Six movements and forty-eight beat pairings explore how different present readers may respond to the wording. The proposed passages remain optional editorial drafts until a current owner and route are verified.

## 2. Story question

Can the story remember courage while refusing to make violence noble, predictable, or a complete explanation of the people the memorial names collectively?

## 5. Human center

The gap between the memorial’s polished account of defense and the private scale of the people flattened into “these brave souls.”
 The deceased or collective subject must never become a device whose only purpose is to teach a clean lesson to the living. Keep the person or people larger than the inscription’s most repeatable line.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **A person who inherits the tribute:** Wants to honor courage but worries that praise can make fear invisible.
- **A careful reader:** Separates the memorial’s claims from a full account of the night.
- **A listener who asks about the plural:** Notices the people compressed into “these brave souls.”

The people listed here are proposed present-day readers, not canon characters and not witnesses unless another verified source establishes that history. Keep the original memorial voice distinct from any reply written for this plan.

## 7. Placement and current reachability

The exact local anchor is memorial_raider_attack/names_in_the_dark. MemorialSystem owns survivor memorialization, grief, mourning, and eulogies, but this separate data record’s loader and player-facing consumer have not been verified. Before content placement, confirm the current schema, owner, route, and presentation context. Catalog presence alone proves no playable route. This plan contributes no new route or content registry.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask who authored a claim, hear a second interpretation, decline to discuss it, or leave without a verdict. These are editorial postures rather than promised controls. Silence must remain a complete response. Do not attach approval, trust, reputation, item, resource, treatment, memorial completion, or relationship state to the reader’s interpretation.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not invent victim or attacker identities, count, tactics, weapons, exact chronology, settlement, outcome, or the meaning of “annihilation.” Do not glorify violence or turn the memorial into a combat tutorial. The text does not establish a playable attack, faction relationship, or security doctrine.
 Keep the source statement, a proposed speaker’s interpretation, and any verified world fact visibly separate. Avoid reenacting death, assigning fault without evidence, or converting grief into a tutorial.

## 11. Narrative sequence

These six editorial movements organize the prose bank; they are not six required visits, a chronology, or a quest chain. A verified consumer may use a single beat, a short excerpt, or none of the candidates.

### Movement 1: The Lasting Claim

“Fought to the last” sounds finished while leaving the actual account absent. In this movement the candidate question is: What does a final-sounding phrase conceal? The memorial’s phrase “fought to the last” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 2: A Night Named, Not Described

The record marks darkness without creating a scene of tactics. In this movement the candidate question is: How can darkness be an emotional setting without becoming combat staging? The memorial’s phrase “raiders” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 3: Bravery in Public

The memorial chooses a public honorific for unnamed people. In this movement the candidate question is: Can praise coexist with vulnerability? The memorial’s phrase “in the night” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 4: Between Us and Annihilation

The sentence frames protection as collective, without explaining the threat. In this movement the candidate question is: Who is included in “us,” and what must remain unknown? The memorial’s phrase “brave souls” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 5: Names in the Dark

The public remembers names, yet the catalog record gives none. In this movement the candidate question is: How does an absent name change the reader’s duty? The memorial’s phrase “stood between us” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 6: The Quiet After Praise

The closing movement lets grief outlast a heroic verdict. In this movement the candidate question is: What does remembrance sound like after the sentence stops? The memorial’s phrase “annihilation” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

## 12. Beat bank and alternative passage drafts

Every movement meets each of eight source phrases. The four forms per pairing are alternatives: an observable scene, an attributable reader-authored fragment, a two-voice conversation, and a conditional return. Their headings help writers compare form; they do not promise that all versions fit one consumer. Dialogue is deliberately from proposed present readers, not from the person memorialized.

### Beat 01: The Lasting Claim × fought to the last

**Beat question:** When a reader meets “fought to the last” during “The Lasting Claim,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a forceful claim that gives no chronology or eyewitness view?

#### Scene draft 001 — fought to the last — The Lasting Claim

This version starts in the small interval between recognizing the record and deciding what to repeat from it. The dramatic work of “The Lasting Claim” comes from placing “fought to the last” beside what the memorial does not say. No invented detail is allowed to close that gap. The proposed reader is not a witness to the life being memorialized; their presence only gives the source wording a present audience.

Let the focus settle on “fought to the last”. The phrase has force because a forceful claim that gives no chronology or eyewitness view. A second person notices that force before anyone turns it into an explanation. They may look toward the line, then toward the person who has stopped reading, giving the beat a human action rather than an invented flashback.

A keeper who questions what praise leaves outside the frame begins with a modest response: “I can read fought to the last, but I cannot tell from this sentence everything that happened. I want to keep the wording because it matters to someone, and I want to leave room where the record leaves room.” The line is written as present interpretation, not a quotation falsely attributed to the person on the memorial.

A listener who pays attention to the missing names replies, “Then let us ask what it asks of us now. When a reader meets “fought to the last” during “The Lasting Claim,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a forceful claim that gives no chronology or eyewitness view? I can honor the claim and still notice what it does not tell us.” The answer should be specific to the source phrase; it must not flatten the disagreement into a lesson about all grief or all survivors.

A third voice, a reader who wants courage remembered without demanding reenactment, may interrupt gently. They do not correct the catalog or add evidence. They ask whether the word “fought to the last” is being repeated because it comforts the room, because it warns the room, or because no one knows what else to say. Let more than one motive remain possible.

The scene’s emotional turn around “fought to the last” in “The Lasting Claim” is small: one listener stops trying to finish the missing story for a moment. A chair shifts, a copied line is turned face down and then face up again, or the speaker chooses a less certain verb. The selected gesture should fit the verified presentation context; no physical memorial site is established by this proposal.

For “fought to the last” in “The Lasting Claim,” player-facing tone stays open. The player may read, ask for the source wording again, decline the conversation, or simply listen. These are writing possibilities, not a promised menu or branching system. No answer earns approval, trust, resources, safety, reputation, or a new relationship state.

The closing breath belongs to the living reader; do not fabricate an answer on behalf of the person commemorated. The last attention returns to “fought to the last”, now heard through the live disagreement rather than treated as a complete biography.

**Drafting focus.** Keep the reader’s attention on “fought to the last” rather than on a newly invented biography. The writer may ask When a reader meets “fought to the last” during “The Lasting Claim,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a forceful claim that gives no chronology or eyewitness view? The answer should emerge through voice, hesitation, and what the speakers decline to claim; it should not be delivered by an omniscient narrator. This restraint is part of the content, not a substitute for dramatic movement.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to the source wording and the present reader’s actual knowledge. Allow one voice to value the memorial and another to object to its universal-sounding conclusion. Neither reaction should be framed as the correct morality. Use quiet material action rather than explanatory narration. Avoid turning illness, hunger, conflict, radiation, cold, machinery, duty, or trust into a puzzle tutorial or instruction to the player.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not invent victim or attacker identities, count, tactics, weapons, exact chronology, settlement, outcome, or the meaning of “annihilation.” Do not glorify violence or turn the memorial into a combat tutorial. The text does not establish a playable attack, faction relationship, or security doctrine.
 Re-check the exact local record and any newer narrative data before selecting text. Retain only passages that the current consumer can reach and attribute honestly. Shortening a candidate is preferable to implying unsupported canon.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed roles are anonymous present-day readers. Do not promote them into named survivors, historical witnesses, patients, faction leaders, victims, or permanent companions without a separate source and authorized content decision. Their relationships exist only within the selected text. Gratitude, skepticism, grief, forgiveness, caution, and disagreement do not become saved relationship values.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified reader-facing owner can stage a present response without claiming a sourced location. **Use a record** when existing content supports authorship and provenance. **Use a conversation** when a second perspective adds a genuine question rather than repeating the thesis. **Use a return vignette** only if the existing route already supports the callback condition; otherwise use its non-conditional final line or omit it. These are writing choices, not feature requests.

|---|---|---|

| Proposed voices | Anonymous roles in this document | Do not claim they are witnesses or canonical characters. |
| Reader response | Four prose alternatives per beat | No new menu, flag, score, reward, relationship, or save behavior. |
| Sensitive subject | tragedy and tags ['raiders', 'defense', 'sacrifice'] | No operational guidance, diagnosis, guarantee, or unsupported outcome. |

The text does not establish a playable attack, faction relationship, or security doctrine.
 Every passage is editorial. The questions of authorship, setting, timing, memory, and consumer remain open until checked against current code and narrative data.
